*Theotokion:* O holy Virgin, Mother of Christ, glory of martyrs, and joy of angels: with them pray unto thy Son To have compassion and save thy servants.

*Ode 3, Heirmos:* Thou art the confirmation of those who flee unto Thee, O Lord; Thou art the light of the darkened, and my spirit hymns Thee.

*Troparion 1:* Thou wast the adornment of sufferers, O wise one, by God-illumined grace; therefore we in the world piously honor thee.

*Troparion 2:* When thy jaws were shattered, O wise martyr, thou didst gratefully sing praise unto our God, Who strengthened thee.

*Troparion 3:* Strengthened by the power of the Divine Spirit, O all-wise one, rejoicing thou didst cast down the serpent who was strong in malice.

*Theotokion:* With mouth and tongue do I confess thee to be the most holy temple of God, O most pure one; therefore deliver me from every affliction.

*Sessional Hymn, Tone 4, Podoben: "Thou Who Wast Raised Up" (Вознесы́йсѧ):*[^424]

Having cast down the exaltations of the enemies like a mighty warrior, O blessed one, thou didst receive the crown of victory. Therefore we praise thee as a valiant sufferer, keeping thy all-festive memory in faith, wherein do thou pray, O much-suffering Hermias, that we be saved from every distress.

*Glory... Both now... Theotokion:*

For after God, O Theotokos, I, humble, flee unto thy divine protection, falling down and praying: Have mercy, O most pure one, for my sins have exceeded my head, and I fear punishment, O Sovereign Lady, and tremble. Make supplication, O pure one, unto thy Son, to deliver me from these.

*Stavrotheotokion:*

She who in the end gave birth in the flesh unto Thee Who wast begotten of the unoriginate Father, seeing Thee hanging upon the Cross, O Christ, cried out: "Woe is me, most beloved Jesus! How is it that Thou, Who art glorified as God by the angels, art now willingly crucified by lawless men, O Son? I hymn Thee, O longsuffering One!"

*Ode 4, Heirmos:* I have heard, O Lord, the mystery of Thy dispensation; I have understood Thy works, and I have glorified Thy Divinity.

*Troparion 1:* The streams of thy blood quenched the fire of polytheism; and unto the faithful they appeared as dew, O passion-bearing martyr worthy of wonder.

*Troparion 2:* Bedewed by the grace of the Trinity, O martyr, thou didst remain unconsumed, standing in the midst of the flames for three days,[^425] O all-glorious one.

*Troparion 3:* The choir of the heavenly hosts looked down from on high upon the courage and the strength of thy struggles, marveling, O sufferer Hermias.

*Theotokion:* O all-hymned Sovereign Lady, beyond word thou didst give birth unto Him Who rests in the saints;

Therefore with hymns we honor thee.

*Ode 5, Heirmos:* Why hast Thou cast me away from Thy presence, O Never-setting Light? And alien darkness hath covered me, wretched one; but turn me back, and direct my ways toward the light of Thy commandments, I pray Thee.

*Troparion 1:* Crushed in body by many tortures, O all-glorious martyr, by grace thou didst bring about the shattering and destruction of the carved idols; therefore, by divine grace, thou healest the spiritual afflictions of all the pious.

*Troparion 2:* Having been made to drink bitter poisons, O martyr, thou didst remain unharmed, calling into the certain faith the giver of them,[^426] who was transformed most radiantly unto the beauty of martyrdom by thy bright working of wonders.

*Troparion 3:* In no wise didst thou turn aside from the superior standing, O blessed one, beholding with simplicity of disposition Him Who strengthened, confirmed, and directed all thy struggles toward Himself.

*Theotokion:* As a spiritual lampstand did the Prophet of old behold thee in the Spirit, O Maiden, bearing the lamp, Christ the Lord, Who with ineffable light enlightens all of us who honor thee in faith, O all-immaculate one.

*Ode 6, Heirmos:* Cleanse me, O Savior, for many are mine iniquities, and lead me up from the depth of evils, I pray Thee; for unto Thee have I cried, and do Thou hear me, O God of my salvation!

*Troparion 1:* Having an earnest desire toward God, O all-blessed one, thou didst endure with joy the pains and tearings of thy divine body, as though another were suffering, beholding the crowns.

*Troparion 2:* Thou didst steadfastly endure the tearing out of thy sinews,[^427] O Hermias, martyr of Christ, by grace tearing out the sinews of the multifaceted enemy, and showing thyself as a pattern of every courage.

*Troparion 3:* Sharpened by the fire of Christ's love, thou wast in truth like a two-edged sword, manifestly cutting down the armies of demons like a steadfast armor-bearer.

*Theotokion:* In a manner fitting God thou feedest Him Who by His beck feeds all things; with thy hands thou holdest Him Who holds all things, O all-immaculate Sovereign Lady; therefore we, the faithful, duly glorify thee.

*Kontakion, Tone 6, Podoben: "Fulfilling the Dispensation for Our Sake" (Є҆́же ѡ҆ на́съ):*[^428]

He that zealously struggled for Christ, and acquired courage in valiant gray hairs, O martyr Hermias: having trampled underfoot the malicious plotting of the lawless by the power of Christ, He remained unharmed by the venomous drinks, crying aloud: "God is with me, and no one is against me!"

*Ode 7, Heirmos:* The Children having arrived from Judea in Babylon of old, by faith in the Trinity trampled down the flame of the furnace, singing: O God of our fathers, blessed art Thou!

*Troparion 1:* Looking with uprightness of mind unto God Who is able to save, thou didst endure the pains of thy valiant body, O Hermias, singing: O God of our fathers, blessed art Thou!

*Troparion 2:* By the sprinkling of thy blood thou didst quench the fire of godlessness, and having entered into the flame thou wast not consumed at all, singing, O most wealthy one: O God of our fathers, blessed art Thou!

*Troparion 3:* Every Church blesses thee, beholding thy valiant deeds, the grace of wonders, and the honors of victory which the Lord granted unto thee, O wise martyr Hermias, who didst struggle brightly.

*Theotokion:* Thou wast adorned with beauty, O Virgin, having given birth unto the beautiful Christ, Who likened Himself unto us and led all the broken up unto life; therefore in faith we ever glorify thee in hymns.

*Ode 8, Heirmos:* Having become victors over the tyrant and the flame by Thy grace, clinging closely unto Thy commandments, the Children cried out: Bless the Lord, all ye works of the Lord!

*Troparion 1:* Strengthened by the true law, O sufferer Hermias, rejoicing thou didst despise the command of the lawless; and through great struggles, O blessed one, thou didst fervently receive the dwellings in the highest unto the ages.

*Troparion 2:* Thou wast not frightened by any surging command of torments, nor didst thou offer honor unto non-existent gods, crying aloud, O all-blessed one: "I honor only the one Jesus the Lord, Who was crucified under Pontius Pilate!"[^429]

*Troparion 3:* Thou didst go to be slaughtered like an innocent sheep, O martyr Hermias, for the sake of Him Who was slaughtered like a lamb for our sake and took away all abominable sacrifices from the earth; therefore in praise we worthily sing of thee.

*Theotokion:* Thou wast revealed to be wider than the heavens, having contained God in thy womb; pray Him unceasingly, O Virgin, to deliver me from the sin that now constrains me, that having expanded in works of piety, I may bless thee in faith.

*Ode 9, Heirmos:* Heaven was dismayed thereat, and the ends of the earth were amazed, that God appeared unto men bodily, and thy womb became more spacious than the heavens; therefore the hierarchies of angels and men magnify thee, O Theotokos.

*Troparion 1:* Having been released from the flesh, O all-lauded passion-bearer, thou wast vouchsafed to behold the good things which God prepared for thee and for those who suffered with thee and cast down the crafty one; with them, pray the Lord that we be saved, O martyr Hermias worthy of praise.

*Troparion 2:* Fair was thy countenance even before thy suffering, but it appeared yet more beautiful after thy marvelous struggles, O wise one, adorned with angelic radiance, O glorious one; therefore thou didst ascend unto the fair bridal chambers, adorned with crowns of victory.

*Troparion 3:* Thou didst despise earthly warfare, and art a soldier for the heavenly King, O wise martyr, by Whose mighty power thou didst sovereignly destroy all the regiments of demons; and having been lawfully crowned with crowns of victory, ever pray for us.

*Troparion 4:* Thy light-bearing memory hath arrived, enlightening our thoughts with the rays of thy steadfast struggles. In it we pray thee, as one who hast boldness before Christ, pray unceasingly that we may obtain mercy on the day of judgment.

*Theotokion:* God, clothed in me, came forth from thee, and clothed me with the garment of incorruption, who by an evil disposition had been stripped of the divine garment. O Theotokos Sovereign Lady, refuge of all mankind, confirmation and wall of the crown-bearing martyrs, and joy of angels!

*(Tailpiece — Decorative floral woodcut vignette marking the conclusion of the Month of May)*[^430]

*(Catchword: Theotokia)*[^431] *(Vacat — Fol. 118v: Unnumbered blank verso)*

# FOOTNOTES AND SCHOLARLY COMMENTARY



[^1]: Jeremiah was cast into the muddy cistern of Malchiah son of the king (Jeremiah 38:6 [LXX 45:6]).

[^2]: Slavonic: "собо́ръ їнле́въ" (typographical contraction for the assembly of the Israelites, referencing their hostility to Jeremiah; cf. Jeremiah 38:6).

[^3]: Cf. Lamentations 4:20: "The breath of our nostrils, the anointed of the Lord, was taken in their pits, of whom we said, Under his shadow we shall live among the heathen."

[^4]: Baruch 3:36–38 (LXX: 3:36–37): "This is our God, and there shall none other be accounted of in comparison of him... Afterward did he shew himself upon earth, and conversed with men." Historically attributed liturgically to the prophecy of Jeremiah.

[^5]: Corinthians pericope 186 corresponds to 2 Corinthians 4:6–15.

[^6]: Luke pericope 14 corresponds to Luke 4:22–30.

[^7]: The name Athanasius (Greek Ἀθανάσιος) derives from athanasia (ἀθανασία), meaning "immortality."

[^8]: St. Germanus of Constantinople refers to St. Athanasius as the "gold-flowing Nile" of Alexandria, evoking both Egyptian geography and his Greek name meaning "immortal."

[^9]: A reference to St. Athanasius's early apologetic treatise Contra Gentes (Against the Heathen / On the Idols), written prior to the Arian controversy.

[^10]: Apollinarianism denied the existence of a rational human soul and mind in Christ, asserting that the Divine Logos took the place of the human spirit. St. Athanasius refuted this in his Tomus ad Antiochenos and letter To Epictetus.

[^11]: Slavonic "А҆́лмазъ" / "А҆дама́нтъ" renders Greek ἀδάμας (adamant, diamond), a classic patristic epithet for St. Athanasius of Alexandria.

[^12]: In this 1761 Pochaiv edition, Fol. 6 (Leaves p11–p12) is bound before Fol. 7 (Leaves p13–p14). Fol. 6r opens abruptly with the third Sticheron at the Praises for St. Athanasius (*By the radiance of thy divine words...*), whereas Fol. 7 contains the preceding portion of Matins (Canon Odes 7–9 and Praises Stichera 1–2). The catchword at the bottom of Fol. 7v (*Слове́съ*) confirms that Fol. 6r immediately succeeds Fol. 7v in liturgical sequence.

[^13]: The printed rubric abbreviation *прⷣ* indicates that the preceding stichera are of the Afterfeast of Mid-Pentecost, while the Doxasticon *Glory* is dedicated to St. Athanasius.

[^14]: St. Theodosius of the Caves († 1074) is commemorated on May 3 (his falling asleep) and August 14 (the translation of his relics). The Pochaiv Menaion places the brief common service to the Holy Martyrs Timothy and Maura first, noting that the full festal service to St. Theodosius follows immediately thereafter (beginning on Leaf p16).

[^15]: The acrostic in Church Slavonic reads: *Тїмо́ѳеа и҆ ма́ѵры болѣ́зни чьствꙋ́ю* ('I honor the sufferings of Timothy and Maura'), composed by St. Joseph the Hymnographer.

[^16]: This phrase directly completes the second troparion of Ode 7 of the Canon of St. Athanasius from Fol. 5v (Leaf p10: *...нече́стїа загради́телѧ [роди́тель, и҆згна́нїа на тѧ̀ воздви́жетъ]*), which was interrupted on Leaf p10 by the binder's transposition of Fol. 6.

[^17]: The Slavonic text reads *королю̀* ('unto the king'), reflecting the Polish-Lithuanian Commonwealth royal prayer formula characteristic of 18th-century Ruthenian Basilian editions printed at the Pochaiv Lavra, rather than the Synodal Russian *цр҃ю̀* ('unto the tsar').

[^18]: This opening phrase on Leaf p15 directly completes the third troparion of Ode 6 of the Canon of Timothy and Maura from Fol. 8v [Leaf p18] (*...любо́вїю до конца̀ вѣнча́вшаго / Хрⷭ҇тꙋ̀ ду́шы приваза́сте...*), due to the inverted gathering in the physical exemplar.

[^19]: According to their Passio, Timothy and Maura hung facing each other on their crosses for nine days and nights before surrendering their spirits to God.

[^20]: This opening phrase on Leaf p17 directly completes the first troparion of Ode 1 of the Canon of Timothy and Maura from Fol. 6v [Leaf p12] (*...ꙗ҆́кѡ да тѣ́хъ / тѣ́хъ мольба́ми ѿ бѣ́дъ и҆зба́вимсѧ*).

[^21]: The Doxasticon at Small Vespers jointly invokes both founding fathers of the Kyiv Caves Lavra, St. Anthony († 1073) and St. Theodosius († 1074), celebrating them as the twin pillars of Rus' monasticism.

[^22]: This clause completes the Doxasticon at "Lord, I have cried" for St. Theodosius from Fol. 10v (Leaf p20).

[^23]: St. Theodosius constructed the great stone Dormition Cathedral of the Kyiv Caves Monastery (consecrated in 1089), dedicated to the Mother of God, establishing the monastery's enduring status as the earthly house of the Theotokos.

[^24]: This sticheron contrasts the spiritual charisms of the two founders of the Kyiv Caves Lavra: St. Anthony, who like Gideon asked for dew and like Elijah brought down fire from heaven, and St. Theodosius, who boldly rebuked Prince Sviatoslav II Yaroslavich of Kyiv for driving out his brother Iziaslav I in 1073.

[^25]: The Kyiv Caves Patericon records that before the construction of the Dormition Cathedral, a miraculous pillar of light and fire extended from earth to heaven, revealing the exact site chosen by the Mother of God for the Lavra's great church.

[^26]: According to the Life of St. Theodosius by St. Nestor the Chronicler, when the saint was brought to church on the eighth day after his birth, the priest, seeing through the Spirit what he would become, named him Theodosius ('given by God') and dedicated him to God's service.

[^27]: In his youth at Kursk, St. Theodosius baked the prosphora (altar breads) with his own hands out of humility and love for the Divine Liturgy, enduring harsh rebuke and beatings from his mother, who deemed such menial manual labor shameful for a youth of noble boyar lineage.

[^28]: St. Theodosius twice attempted to make a pilgrimage to the Holy Sepulchre in Jerusalem, but was turned back each time by divine dispensation to fulfill his destiny as the founder of Rus' monasticism in Kyiv.

[^29]: St. Theodosius secretly wore heavy iron chains around his loins from his youth. When his mother discovered them clinging to his flesh and soaked in dried blood, she tore them off him in anger and beat him severely.

[^30]: After tracking down Theodosius at the Kyiv Caves, his mother was so moved by his spiritual exhortations and filial love that she renounced the world herself, took the monastic habit at the Monastery of St. Nicholas in Kyiv, and spent the remainder of her days in ascetic repentance.

[^31]: The Kyiv Caves Patericon relates that while St. Theodosius was travelling on monastery business, a nobleman's servant mistook the shabbily dressed hegumen for a common laborer, commanded him to get into the wagon and drive the horses while the servant slept, only for the servant to be terrified upon entering Kyiv to see princes and boyars dismounting to bow low before Theodosius.

[^32]: The Kyiv Caves Patericon relates that when the monastery's cellarer was despairing over having no gold to buy provisions for the brethren, an unknown handsome youth appeared in the courtyard, handed St. Theodosius a bag containing three bags of gold ducats, saying it was sent by God, and immediately vanished.

[^33]: The Kyiv Caves Patericon relates that when Grand Prince Iziaslav Yaroslavich came to the monastery to dine with St. Theodosius, the cellarer informed the hegumen that there was no more mead in the cellars; but St. Theodosius commanded him to go back and check the vessels in faith, whereupon they were found miraculously filled to the brim with sweet mead.

[^34]: The title 'capital city of Rus'' (Slavonic: *началограде росскїй*) designates Kyiv, the cradle of Eastern Slavic Christianity and monastic life, extolling St. Theodosius as the spiritual father and protector of the mother of Rus' cities.

[^35]: The Kyiv Caves Patericon relates that a band of robbers came by night to rob the monastery church of the Dormition, but were astounded to see the church lifted high off the ground, floating in the air, while angelic singing resounded from within. Terrified by the miracle, the robbers fled in awe, later came to St. Theodosius in repentance, and embraced monastic tonsure.

[^36]: The appointed liturgical readings for St. Theodosius are Hebrews 13:7–16 (pericope 334) and Matthew 11:27–30 (pericope 47), standard festal lections for venerable monastic founders.

[^37]: The physical Pochaiv 1761 edition contains a typographical metathesis: *ѿ ненаикисоꙋ́мѡжныя* (read *ѿ неискусомужныя*, 'from her that knew not man', referring to the Virgin Mother of God).

[^38]: The Slavonic acrostic reads: *Пелагі́и хвалꙋ̀ и҆́стиннꙋ плету̀* ('I weave a true praise for Pelagia'), corresponding to the Greek acrostic of the canon composed by St. Theophanes the Branded in Tone 4.

[^39]: The hymn refers to St. Pelagia's rejection of the imperial bridal robes brought to her from the son of Emperor Diocletian. After receiving holy baptism (the 'bath of regeneration', Titus 3:5) from Bishop Linus of Tarsus on Mount Taurus, she distributed her luxurious garments to the poor and clothed herself in white baptismal robes.

[^40]: St. Pelagia of Tarsus was condemned by Emperor Diocletian to be burned alive within a red-hot bronze/copper bull (Slavonic: *конобъ*, Greek: βοῦς χαλκοῦς). Entering the blazing vessel voluntarily after making the sign of the Cross, she surrendered her soul unto Christ as a sweet-smelling whole burnt offering.

[^41]: St. Irene the Great-Martyr of Magedon (1st–2nd c.), born Penelope, daughter of the pagan prince Licinius in Persia or Thrace, was instructed in the Christian faith by her tutor Apellian and baptized by St. Timothy, disciple of the Apostle Paul.

[^42]: The Slavonic acrostic reads: *Твои́ми мя̀ мл҃твами мꙋ́нце і҆ри́но покры́й* ('Shield me by thy prayers, O martyr Irene'), representing the Greek canon composed by St. Joseph the Hymnographer.

[^43]: A theological wordplay upon the martyr's name Irene (from the Greek Εἰρήνη, 'peace', Slavonic: *миръ*), celebrated here as *мироименитая* ('named for peace'), whose endurance brought the peace of Christ to pagans and tormentors alike.

[^44]: In the Ode 6 Theotokion, the printed Slavonic text *Про́бли* represents an abbreviated or defective form for *Проѡбразѝ* (Greek: προετύπωσε, 'prefigured'), alluding to Moses the God-seer beholding the burning bush on Mount Sinai unconsumed (Exodus 3:2).

[^45]: On May 6 the Ruthenian Church celebrates the commemoration of the Holy and Righteous Job the Long-Suffering, whose endurance amid bodily affliction and catastrophic bereavement stands as an Old Testament archetype of Christ's Passion.

[^46]: The Slavonic acrostic reads: *Многострада́льнаго досто́йнѡ пѣ́ти і҆́ѡва* ('Worthily to hymn the long-suffering Job'), without the Theotokia, corresponding to the Greek canon composed by St. Joseph the Hymnographer.

[^47]: An allusion to the theophany in Job 42:5–6: 'I had heard of Thee by the hearing of the ear, but now mine eye sees Thee; therefore I despise myself, and repent in dust and ashes.'

[^48]: The Epistle lection appointed for Holy and Righteous Job is Galatians 5:22–6:2 (pericope 213), extolling the fruit of the Spirit and the bearing of one another's burdens as the fulfillment of the law of Christ.

[^49]: The Gospel lection appointed for Holy and Righteous Job is Matthew 11:27–30 (pericope 43), the invitation of the Savior to take His yoke and find rest for souls.

[^50]: On May 7 the Church commemorates the miraculous apparition of the luminous Sign of the Precious Cross over Jerusalem on May 7, 351 A.D., stretching from Golgotha to the Mount of Olives, witnessed by the entire population and described in the famous encyclical letter of St. Cyril of Jerusalem to Emperor Constantius II.

[^51]: The Pochaiv 1761 edition witnesses the distinct Ruthenian recension formula *и҆ вѣ́рнаго на́шего короля̀* ('and our faithful king', referring to Augustus III of the Polish-Lithuanian Commonwealth), whereas Russian Synodal editions read *вѣрнаго императора нашего* or *благовѣрныхъ правителей*.

[^52]: A theological wordplay upon the name of the martyr Acacius (Greek: Ἀκάκιος, from ἀκακία, 'guilelessness, innocence', Slavonic: *безѕло́бїе*), celebrating St. Acacius the Centurion of Cappadocia, martyred in Byzantium (303 A.D.).

[^53]: St. Acacius (a Cappadocian centurion martyred in Byzantium under Maximian in 303 A.D.) is celebrated throughout this canon with recurring wordplays on his name (Greek: Ἀκάκιος, 'guileless, innocent', Slavonic: *неѕло́бивъ*).

[^54]: An epigraphic witness to Constantinople ('great among cities and reigning over all'), where St. Acacius the Centurion was martyred. Emperor Constantine subsequently erected a celebrated martyrium church in his honor at the *Heptaskalon*, which was regarded as one of the chief protective shrines and 'crowns' of the imperial capital.

[^55]: On May 7, the Menaion prescribes the Matins canon of the luminous apparition of the Precious Cross to be combined with the canon of the prevailing festal period from the Pentecostarion (e.g., Mid-Pentecost or Pascha), appointing the Pentecostarion canon with the heirmos to 6, and the canon of the Precious Cross to 6.

[^56]: The Ikos of May 7 links the 351 A.D. apparition of the Cross over Jerusalem to the historic vision of Constantine the Great before the Battle of the Milvian Bridge (312 A.D.), proclaiming the Cross as the perpetual protective sign and 'weapon of peace' granted to the Christian empire.

[^57]: The Epistle lection appointed for the Apparition of the Cross is 1 Corinthians 1:18–24 (pericope 125): 'For the word of the Cross is folly to those who are perishing, but to us who are being saved it is the power of God.'

[^58]: The Gospel lection appointed for the Apparition of the Cross is John 19:6–11, 13–20, 25–28, 30–35 (pericope 60), the composite Passion Gospel describing Christ before Pilate, the Crucifixion at Golgotha, and the piercing of His side.

[^59]: May 8 commemorates the Holy Apostle and Evangelist John the Theologian, specifically celebrating the annual miracle in Ephesus where a fine, fragrant, healing dust or 'manna' (*hē konis*) was emitted from his tomb, through which the sick were delivered from various infirmities.

[^60]: St. Arsenius the Great (c. 354–445 A.D.), a Roman senator and tutor to the sons of Emperor Theodosius the Great (Arcadius and Honorius), who renounced the imperial court of Constantinople to embrace radical solitude, fasting, and weeping in the Egyptian desert of Scetis.

[^61]: The first festal Paremia appointed for St. John the Theologian at Great Vespers is 1 John 3:21–4:6, declaring confidence toward God, the commandment of mutual love, and the testing of spirits by the confession of Christ come in the flesh.

[^62]: The second festal Paremia appointed for St. John the Theologian is 1 John 4:11–16, testifying to the mutual love of Christians as the dwelling of God within them and the contemplation of God through the Holy Spirit.

[^63]: The third festal Paremia appointed for St. John the Theologian is 1 John 4:20–5:5, declaring that he who loves God must love his brother also, and that faith in Jesus as the Son of God is the victory that overcomes the world.

[^64]: St. Andrew Pyros (also known as Andrew of Jerusalem or Andrew the Blind, 8th c.), a prolific Byzantine hymnographer who composed numerous idiomelon stichera for major festal liturgies.

[^65]: St. John the Monk (St. John of Damascus, c. 676–749 A.D.), author of the Octoechos and numerous festal idiomelon stichera.

[^66]: An explicit patristic refutation of Arianism: Arius claimed of the Son *ἦν ποτε ὅτε οὐκ ἦν* ('there was once when He was not'), whereas St. John the Theologian affirmed *'In the beginning was the Word'* (John 1:1), thus casting down the heretical assertion.

[^67]: The Matins Gospel appointed for St. John the Theologian is John 21:15–25 (pericope 67), Christ's post-resurrection dialogue with Peter ('Feed My sheep') and the mysterious prophecy regarding the beloved disciple: 'If I will that he tarry till I come, what is that to thee?'

[^68]: Christ bestowed upon St. John and his brother James the surname *Boanerges*, which is 'Sons of Thunder' (Mark 3:17), prefiguring St. John's thundering declaration of the pre-eternal divinity of the Logos.

[^69]: An allusion to the *Apophthegmata Patrum* (Sayings of the Desert Fathers, *Arsenius* 13), wherein Abba Arsenius taught that an hour's sleep suffices for a monastic warrior, and would spend whole nights in vigils until sleep overtook him by sheer natural necessity.

[^70]: A direct quotation of John 1:17: 'For the law was given by Moses, but grace and truth came by Jesus Christ.'

[^71]: An allusion to Isaiah 6:6–7, where one of the Seraphim touched the prophet's lips with a live coal taken with tongs from off the altar, cleansing his iniquity and fitting him for prophetic ministry.

[^72]: A direct citation of John 1:18: 'No man hath seen God at any time; the only begotten Son, which is in the bosom of the Father, he hath declared him.'

[^73]: An allusion to the *Apophthegmata Patrum* (*Arsenius* 30), recounting that Abba Arsenius would stand from Saturday evening in vigil with arms outstretched toward heaven until the morning sun shone upon his face on Sunday.

[^74]: Canticle of Canticles (Song of Songs) 3:7: 'Behold his bed, which is Solomon's; threescore valiant men are about it, of the valiant of Israel.'

[^75]: An allusion to the *Apophthegmata Patrum* (*Arsenius* 18), where St. Arsenius, having formerly used sweet perfumes in the imperial palace, mortified his senses by working with palm fronds steeped in water that had grown fetid, offering it as an ascetic counter-balance.

[^76]: John 19:26–27, Christ's entrustment from the Cross of His Mother to the virgin disciple: 'Woman, behold thy son! Then says he to the disciple, Behold thy mother!'

[^77]: A reference to the miraculous vision in the *Apophthegmata Patrum* (*Arsenius* 27), wherein a brother peering through the window of Abba Arsenius's cell saw the venerable elder standing wholly transformed into fire like a flame.

[^78]: Psalm 44:1 (LXX): 'Mine heart hath poured forth a good word' (*Ѿры́гнꙋ се́рдце моѐ сло́во бл҃го*), patristically interpreted as the unoriginate generation of the Son from the Father.

[^79]: An allusion to John 19:34, where the soldier pierced Christ's side with a spear, and immediately there came out blood and water, witnessed and recorded by St. John the Evangelist.

[^80]: Psalm 18:5 (LXX) / Psalm 19:4, the standard apostolic prokeimenon: 'Their sound hath gone forth into all the earth, and their words unto the ends of the world.'

[^81]: 1 John 1:1–7 (Apostolos pericope 73), appointed for the feasts of St. John the Theologian.

[^82]: Psalm 88:6 (LXX) / Psalm 89:5: 'The heavens shall confess Thy wonders, O Lord; and Thy truth in the congregation of the saints.'

[^83]: In the Byzantine liturgical tradition, 'More honorable than the Cherubim' is omitted at Ode 9 throughout the Paschal season and during Major feasts of the Lord, being replaced by the Festal Refrains and the Heirmos of Ode 9.

[^84]: The liturgical reading from the Apocalypse of St. John is traditionally appointed to be read during the All-Night Vigil of the Apostle at the Blessing of the Loaves (Artoklasia), in keeping with the ancient monastic practice of reading his Revelation.

[^85]: The Forefeast Canon of Ascension (normally sung on Wednesday of the Apodosis of Pascha) is transferred to Tuesday so that the Canon of the Apostle John may be chanted alongside Pascha and the Blind Man.

[^86]: The 318 God-bearing Fathers of the First Ecumenical Council of Nicaea (325 A.D.), commemorated on the Seventh Sunday after Pascha (the Sunday between Ascension and Pentecost).

[^87]: In Slavonic Menaia of the Ruthenian and Russian traditions, the full festal service for the Translation of the Relics of St. Nicholas from Myra in Lycia to Bari in Apulia (May 9, 1087) is printed as a distinct, complete Vigil office immediately following the shorter common commemoration of the Prophet Isaiah and Martyr Christopher.

[^88]: An allusion to Isaiah 6:6–7, the seraph touching the prophet's lips with a live coal from the altar, patristically interpreted as a type of the Incarnate Christ in the Holy Eucharist.

[^89]: Isaiah 6:1: 'I saw also the Lord sitting upon a throne, high and lifted up, and His train filled the temple.'

[^90]: The name *Christopher* (Greek: Χριστοφόρος) signifies 'Christ-bearer', here highlighted in the hymnography as 'pillar named after Christ' (*хрїстоимени́тый сто́лпе*).

[^91]: Exodus 15:1ff., the Song of Moses at the Red Sea, the scriptural foundation for Ode 1 of the Canon.

[^92]: Isaiah 11:1: 'And there shall come forth a rod out of the stem of Jesse, and a Branch [flower] shall grow out of his roots.'

[^93]: 1 Kings 2:4 (LXX) / 1 Samuel 2:4, the Song of Hannah, the scriptural foundation for Ode 3 of the Canon: 'The bow of the mighty men is broken, and they that stumbled are girded with strength.'

[^94]: Isaiah 28:16: 'Behold, I lay in Zion for a foundation a stone, a tried stone, a precious corner stone, a sure foundation.'

[^95]: Isaiah 7:14: 'Behold, a virgin shall conceive, and bear a son, and shall call his name Immanuel.'

[^96]: Isaiah 9:6: 'For unto us a child is born, unto us a son is given: and the government shall be upon his shoulder.'

[^97]: Habakkuk 3:2ff., the Prayer of Habakkuk, the scriptural foundation for Ode 4 of the Canon.

[^98]: Isaiah 41:1: 'Keep silence before me, O islands; and let the people renew their strength' (*Ѡ҆бнови́тесѧ ко мнѣ̀ ѻ҆́строви*).

[^99]: Isaiah 8:3; cf. Isaiah 9:6.

[^100]: Isaiah 9:6.

[^128]: The petition for the king (*королю нашему*) reflects the historical publication of this Menaion at the Pochaiv Lavra within the Polish-Lithuanian Commonwealth.

[^129]: Cf. Isaiah 52:7; Romans 10:15: "How beautiful are the feet of them that preach the gospel of peace, and bring glad tidings of good things!"

[^136]: Church Slavonic: въ рѡссі́и (*v Rossii*), referring here to the historical lands of Rus' (Ruthenia) where the feast of the Translation of the relics of Saint Nicholas to Bari was instituted in 1089 and widely observed.

[^137]: An allusion to the traditional miracle recorded in the Vita of Saint Nicholas (*Praxis de stratelatis*), wherein the saint appeared in a dream to Emperor Constantine the Great and Prefect Ablabius to deliver three innocent Roman military officers from unjust execution.

[^138]: Cf. 1 Timothy 3:15; Daniel 9:23.

[^146]: Simon is called "the Zealot" (Greek: Ζηλωτής, *Zēlōtēs*; Aramaic: *Qananaia*, Matthew 10:4; Luke 6:15; Acts 1:13).

[^147]: The three readings prescribed are the Catholic Epistles of Saint John the Theologian from May 8: 1 John 1:1–7; 1 John 2:7–17; 1 John 3:21–4:6.

[^178]: Exodus 15:6 (LXX): "Thy right hand, O Lord, is glorified in power; Thy right hand, O Lord, hath shattered the enemy."

[^179]: Luke 24:49: "Until ye be endued with power from on high."

[^180]: Refers to Patriarch Germanus I of Constantinople, whose father, the patrician Justinian, was executed by Emperor Constantine IV Pogonatus. Germanus was forcibly castrated and enrolled among the clergy of the Great Church (Hagia Sophia).

[^181]: Exodus 3:5: "Loose thy shoes from off thy feet; for the place whereon thou standest is holy ground."

[^182]: Refers to the Byzantine Iconoclast Emperor Leo III the Isaurian (reigned 717–741), who deposed Patriarch Germanus I of Constantinople in 730 for refusing to endorse imperial iconoclasm.

[^183]: Habakkuk 3:3 (LXX): "God shall come from Teman, and the Holy One from the dark and shady mountain."

[^184]: In the Life of Saint Epiphanius, the saint miraculously turned water into wine to succor laborers fainting from extreme heat and thirst.

[^185]: Psalm 43:23 (LXX): "For Thy sake we are killed all the day long; we are accounted as sheep for the slaughter."

[^186]: In the Life of Saint Epiphanius, an Arab chieftain struck the saint and was immediately struck blind; upon repentance, Epiphanius prayed and restored sight to his eye.

[^187]: Exodus 7:1: "And the Lord said unto Moses, See, I have made thee a god to Pharaoh."

[^188]: 1 Kings (1 Samuel) 2:30: "Them that honor Me I will honor, and they that despise Me shall be lightly esteemed."

[^189]: Psalm 83:6, 8 (LXX): "In his heart he has disposed to ascend... They shall go from strength to strength."

[^190]: Daniel 3:23–26: The salvation of Ananias, Azarias, and Misael from the Babylonian fiery furnace.

[^286]: The Slavonic printed text contains the typographical error *ст҃і́и му́нцы* for *ст҃і́и мꙋ́ченицы* (holy martyrs).

[^287]: The Pochaiv witness reads *Пе́спою* (typographical distortion) for *Пѣ́снїю* (or *Пе́сньми*).

[^288]: The printed Pochaiv text reads *пога́влавше*, an epigraphic corruption/typesetting error for *погла́вльше* (holding as chief) or *поба́вльше*.

[^289]: The Pochaiv text again has the contraction or misprint *мꙋ́нцы* for *мꙋ́ченицы*.

[^290]: Referring to the conversion and martyrdom of Dionysius and Andrew, moved by the steadfast witness and virginal purity of St. Christina.

[^291]: Slavonic *Семосвѣ́тлагѡ*, an error for *Седмосвѣ́тлагѡ* (seven-branched / seven-lighted), referencing the seven holy virgin martyrs (Tecusa, Alexandra, Claudia, Phaina, Euphrasia, Matrona, and Julia).

[^292]: Alluding to Heb. 12:23: "To the general assembly and church of the firstborn, which are written in heaven." Note also the Slavonic printed error *пе́рбенецъ* for *пе́рвенецъ*.

[^293]: The Slavonic printed text reads *съ мꙋ́нки*, an epigraphic/recensional syncopation for *съ мꙋ́ченики* (with the martyrs).

[^294]: The form *мꙋ́нкъ* in the Pochaiv witness represents *мꙋ́ченикъ* (martyrs).

[^295]: The printed Slavonic witness reads `смꙋ́драго` (*smudrago*), an epigraphic misprint for `мꙋ́драго` (*mudrago*, "wise").

[^296]: The printed Slavonic witness reads `великонмени́тїи` (*velikonmenitii*), an epigraphic misprint with Cyrillic 'н' for 'и' (`великоимени́тїи`, "renowned / illustrious").

[^297]: The printed Slavonic witness reads `Сꙗ҆́ческими` (*Syacheskimi*), an epigraphic variant or misprint for `Вся́ческими` (*Vsyacheskimi*, "with all-various / all manner of").

[^298]: Fol. 77v: The printed Slavonic witness reads `бнтїйствꙋя` (*bntiistvuya*), an epigraphic misprint with Cyrillic 'б' for 'в' (`витїйствꙋя`, "rhetorically / eloquently proclaiming").

[^299]: Fol. 77v: The printed text reads `за млⷭ҇рдїе млⷭ҇ти`, an epigraphic corruption or pleonasm for `за милосе́рдїе ми́лости` ("out of the tenderheartedness of mercy").

[^300]: Fol. 78r: The printed Slavonic witness reads `ᲂу҆слы́шался` (*uslyshalsya*), an epigraphic corruption for `озари́вся` / Greek `καταυγασθείς` ("having been illumined / shone upon").

[^301]: Fol. 78r: The printed Slavonic text reads `Въ со́нномъ` (*V sonnom*), an epigraphic scribal error for `Въ со́нмѣ` (*V sonme*, "in the assembly / choir").

[^302]: Fol. 78v: `Ѡ҆нтїйскꙋю` refers to Julius Annius (Anthus), the Roman proconsul of Prusa under Diocletian, who cast Saint Patrick and his companions into the boiling thermal waters of Prusa.

[^303]: Fol. 78v: The printed Slavonic witness reads `дв҃а` (*dva*), an epigraphic scribal error/contraction for `дв҃да` (*Dvda* / *Davida*, King David; cf. Ps. 44:13 LXX).

[^304]: Fol. 79r: The printed Slavonic witness reads `ст҃ли` (*stli*), an epigraphic contraction for `ст҃и́тели` (*stíteli*, "hierarchs").

[^305]: Fol. 80r: The printed Slavonic text reads `къ ве́нѣй высотѣ̀` (*k venei vysote*), an epigraphic corruption for `къ оной высотѣ` or `къ горней высотѣ` ("unto that / heavenly height").

[^306]: Fol. 80v: The printed Slavonic text reads `ѡ҆лꙋчи́лся є҆сѝ` (*oluchilsya yesi*), an epigraphic misprint for `ѡ҆полчи́лся є҆сѝ` / `ᲂу҆стреми́лся є҆сѝ` ("thou didst arm thyself / hasten").

[^307]: Leaf p161 opens mid-troparion completing the final Ode 9 troparion of the Canon to Holy Martyr Thalelaeus begun on physical leaf p160 (Page_0160.jpg).

[^308]: Leaf p162 concludes mid-phrase at the end of the first half of the Ode 9 Heirmos (дв҃и́ческагѡ [чре́ва]), completed on leaf p163 (Page_0163.jpg).

[^309]: Slavonic: Ст҃ы́хъ вели́кихъ цре́й, и҆ равноапо́столѡвъ, кѡнстанти́на, и҆ є҆ле́ны. The imperial titles are translated diplomatically according to Master Translation Standard MTS-1.

[^310]: The Pochaiv print rubric specifies: Стїхи̑ры, пра́здника, на ѕ҃: і҆ ст҃ы́хъ, на є҃. (Stichera of the Feast on 6, and of the Saints on 5). Although standard Byzantine and Ruthenian Typika prescribe a total of 10 Stichera at Great Vespers (typically Feast on 6 and Saints on 4, or 5 and 5), the printed numeral є҃ (5) is witnessed in physical ink.

[^311]: Third Book of Kings in Septuagint reckoning (1 Kings 8:22–23, 27–30).

[^312]: Isaiah 61:10–62:5. In the Pochaiv 1761 edition, the opening words of this reading are printed on a small cartouche/cancel slip pasted over the lower margin of Fol. 82v (Page_0164.jpg), concluding with the catchword возложи́, which connects directly to the top of Fol. 83r (Page_0165.jpg).

[^313]: Isaiah 60:1–16.

[^314]: The rubric directs the singing of the Sticheron of the patronal dedication of the temple first, followed by the idiomelon stichera of the saints.

[^315]: Melodic model: Tone 2, Podoben: "When from the Tree" (Yegda ot dreva).

[^316]: Psalm 88:20b [LXX 88:19b; MT 89:19b–20a].

[^317]: Psalm 44:8a [LXX 44:7a; MT 45:7a].

[^318]: The Troparion of St. Constantine (Tone 8) is one of the foundational royal troparia of the Byzantine and Ruthenian liturgies.

[^319]: Melodic model: Tone 4, Podoben: "Speedily Anticipate" (Skoro predvari).

[^320]: The Matins Gospel is John Pericope 36 (John 10:9–16), the standard Festal Gospel for hierarchs and equal-to-the-apostles sovereigns.

[^321]: Physical leaf coordinate: p166 (Page_0166.jpg, Fol. 83v). The Slavonic rubric reads: "и҆щѝ на лїті́и. ли́стъ, пг на ѡ҆боро́тѣ" (seek at the Litiya, folio 83 on the reverse).

[^322]: Melodic model: Tone 8, Podoben: "Of Wisdom" (Premudrosti).

[^323]: In physical ink, the phrase "и҆з̾ ро́ба поги́бели" witnesses the dialectal/epigraphic phonetic interchange of б for в (i.e., ро́ва, the pit of destruction).

[^324]: By Patriarch Methodius of Constantinople (843–847).

[^325]: Galatians 1:11–19 (Pericope 200).

[^326]: Acts 18:22–28 (Pericope 44).

[^327]: John 10:1–9 (Pericope 35 from the half).

[^328]: This rubric establishes the precedence and typikon order when the feast of Constantine and Helena coincides with Ascension Thursday or the Sunday of the Holy Fathers of the First Ecumenical Council (the Seventh Sunday of Pascha).

[^329]: The Pochaiv print contains the typographical error `ше́ствїа ненла̑` for `ше́ствїѧ на нб҃са` (heavenly journeys/ascents).

[^330]: Fol. 80 corresponds to physical leaf p159 (Page_0159.jpg), May 20.

[^331]: By Saint Joseph the Hymnographer (c. 816–886). The acrostic in Greek and Slavonic spells out: "The most great Basiliscus I praise. Joseph."

[^332]: Saint Michael the Confessor, Bishop of Synnada in Phrygia (d. ca. 826), zealous confessor of Orthodoxy and defender of the holy icons during the iconoclast persecution under Emperor Leo V the Armenian.

[^333]: Physical leaf p180 (Page_0180.jpg, Fol. 90v) concludes mid-sticheron on the catchword "стѧжа́хъ" ("have I acquired"), completed on physical leaf p181 in Cohort 19.

[^334]: Physical leaf p181 completes the final Sticheron of the Theotokos from leaf p180 (Fol. 90v), continuing from the catchword "стѧжа́хъ" ("have I acquired").

[^335]: The initial phrase of this alternate Theotokion is printed partly in red ink in the margin: "Гла́сы велича́ю та̀ * ра́достными чи́стаѧ" ("With voices I magnify thee, with joyful lips, O Pure One...").

[^336]: Ode 1 Heirmos: cf. Exodus 15:1–19 (Song of Moses).

[^337]: Ode 3 Heirmos: cf. 1 Samuel 2:1–2 (Song of Hannah, LXX 1 Kingdoms 2:1–2).

[^338]: Ode 4 Heirmos: cf. Habakkuk 3:1–19 (Song of Habakkuk, LXX).

[^339]: Psalm 44:2 (LXX): "My tongue is the pen of a quickly writing scribe."

[^340]: 1 Corinthians 4:9: "For we are made a spectacle unto the world, and to angels, and to men."

[^341]: Ode 6 Heirmos: cf. Jonah 2:2–9 (Song of Jonah).

[^342]: St. Michael opposed the iconoclasts, who were associated by Orthodox defenders with the heresy of Mani (the Manichaeans) for denying the physical reality and depictability of Christ's incarnate human nature.

[^343]: St. Basil the Great, *On the Holy Spirit*, 18.45: "For the honor paid to the icon passes over to the prototype." This patristic maxim was affirmed as the dogmatic cornerstone of the Seventh Ecumenical Council (Nicaea II, 787).

[^344]: Gideon's fleece; cf. Judges 6:36–40, Psalm 71:6 (LXX).

[^345]: St. Simeon Stylites the Younger (521–592), who lived upon a pillar on the Marvelous/Wonderful Mountain (Mons Admirabilis) near Antioch.

[^346]: By Patriarch Germanus I of Constantinople (ca. 634–733/740).

[^347]: Isaiah 6:6: "Then flew one of the seraphims unto me, having a live coal in his hand..."

[^348]: The acrostic of the canon in the Slavonic text reads: "Чꙋ́дне пою̀ Сѷмеѡ́не твою̀ бл҃года́ть" ("I hymn thy grace, O marvelous Simeon").

[^349]: Titus 3:5: "Not by works of righteousness which we have done, but according to His mercy He saved us, by the washing of regeneration, and renewing of the Holy Ghost."

[^350]: Psalm 86:3 (LXX): "Glorious things are spoken of thee, O city of God."

[^351]: In the hagiographical tradition of St. Simeon Stylites the Younger, an angel of the Lord appeared to him during his childhood, instructing him in ascetic life and the way of angelic contemplation.

[^352]: Ode 4 Heirmos: cf. Habakkuk 3:1–19 (Song of Habakkuk, LXX).

[^353]: Song of Songs 2:2: "As the lily among thorns, so is my love among the daughters."

[^354]: Physical leaf p190 (Fol. 95v) concludes mid-sentence at the end of the first stanza of the Ikos with the catchword "принесо́мъ" ("let us offer"), completed on physical leaf p191 in Cohort 20.

[^355]: Ode 7 Heirmos: cf. Daniel 3:19–25, 49–50; Song of the Three Holy Youths (LXX Daniel 3:26–45, 52–56).

[^356]: St. Simeon established his monastery upon the mountain called the Marvelous Mountain (Mons Admirabilis, or Marvelous Mountain), situated southwest of Antioch near Seleucia Pieria.

[^357]: Ode 8 Heirmos: cf. Daniel 3:19–20; Song of the Three Holy Youths (LXX Daniel 3:57–88).

[^358]: Ode 9 Heirmos: cf. Habakkuk 3:2; Luke 1:48.

[^359]: An allusion to the Gospel miracle of the multiplication of loaves (Matthew 14:15–21; 15:32–38) mirrored in the life of St. Simeon, who multiplied wheat in the monastery's empty granaries during a time of famine.

[^360]: St. Martha, mother of St. Simeon the Stylite (commemorated July 4), who frequently interceded for the citizens of Antioch and Seleucia.

[^361]: Colossians 1:15: "Who is the image of the invisible God, the firstborn of every creature."

[^362]: The Stavrotheotokion addresses the Mystery of the Crucifixion: Christ is God impassible according to His divine nature, yet suffered in the flesh for the salvation of all.

[^363]: The Third Finding of the Honorable Head of the Holy Prophet, Forerunner, and Baptist John (traditionally dated to ca. 850, when the head was transferred from Comana in Cappadocia to Constantinople under Emperor Michael III and Patriarch Ignatius).

[^364]: St. John the Baptist's head was translated from Comana to Constantinople around 850 during the reign of Emperor Michael III and his mother Empress Theodora, in the patriarchate of St. Ignatius.

[^365]: Reading 1 at Great Vespers: composite prophetical reading from Isaiah 40:1–3, 9; 41:17–18; 45:8; 48:20; 54:1.

[^366]: Reading 2 at Great Vespers: Malachi 3:1–3, 5–7, 12, 17–18; 4:4–6 (LXX 3:22–24).

[^367]: Reading 3 at Great Vespers: Wisdom of Solomon 4:7, 16, 17, 19–20; 5:1–5.

[^368]: Psalm 131:17 (LXX).

[^369]: Psalm 131:1 (LXX).

[^370]: Fol. 99r–99v (physical leaves p197–p198) contains a physical paper repair patch at the lower outer corner, partially obscuring the printed Theotokion on Fol. 99r (col. 2) and the initial of the Ode 1 Heirmos on Fol. 99v (col. 1). The bracketed portions are editorially restored from the surviving letter-traces visible beneath the repair tissue and the standard Slavonic text of the Theotokion and Heirmos.

[^371]: John 1:29: "Behold the Lamb of God, which takes away the sin of the world."

[^372]: Ode 1 Heirmos: cf. Exodus 14:21–28; 15:1–19.

[^373]: Luke 1:20, 64: Zechariah's dumbness was loosed at the naming of John.

[^374]: Matthew 3:13–17; Mark 1:9–11: John laid his hand upon Christ (the Head of the Church, Ephesians 1:22; Colossians 1:18) at His baptism in the Jordan, where the Holy Trinity was revealed.

[^375]: In the First and Second Findings, the Baptist's head was preserved in an earthen vessel (jar); in the Third Finding at Comana, it was found enclosed in a silver vessel hidden in the earth.

[^376]: The translation of the head to Constantinople ("the imperial city").

[^377]: Ode 3 Heirmos: an ancient byzantine heirmos composed by St. John Damascene.

[^378]: The contrast between Salome's wanton dance before Herod (Matthew 14:6–11) and the reverent entry of the Baptist's head into the church at Constantinople carried by Patriarch Ignatius.

[^379]: Emperor Michael III and Patriarch Ignatius met the procession carrying the head of the Baptist into the capital city.

[^380]: Ode 4 Heirmos: cf. Habakkuk 3:2 (LXX).

[^381]: Luke 1:17: "And he shall go before Him in the spirit and power of Elias."

[^382]: Ode 5 Heirmos: cf. Psalm 42:2; Isaiah 26:9.

[^383]: Comana in Pontus (Cappadocia), where the precious head had been concealed during the period of iconoclastic persecution before its third finding ca. 850.

[^384]: "The hierarch sharing the name of the God-bearer" (*бг҃оно́сца тезоимени́тый*) designates Patriarch Ignatius of Constantinople (847–858, 867–877), named after St. Ignatius the God-bearer (Theophoros) of Antioch.

[^385]: Ode 6 Heirmos: cf. Jonah 2:2–9.

[^386]: The reference to the "bitter winter of heresies" (*є҆ресе́й разорѝ го́рькꙋю зи́мꙋ*) commemorates the final overthrow of the iconoclast heresy in 843 under Empress Theodora and the subsequent flourishing of veneration of relics and icons.

[^387]: The Pochaiv 1761 edition witnesses the petition for the reigning Polish monarch ("unto our king", *коро́лю на́шемꙋ*, referring to King Augustus III of the Polish-Lithuanian Commonwealth, r. 1734–1763), reflecting the Ruthenian Uniate recension printed within the Crown of Poland.

[^388]: The 1761 Pochaiv print labels this troparion as *Бг҃оро:* (*Theotokion*), though its text addresses the Forerunner regarding Christ's Incarnation from virginal blood and the preaching of light to those in Hades.

[^389]: The 'sacredly ministering shepherd' (сщ҃еннодѣ́йственномꙋ па́стырю) refers to Patriarch Ignatius of Constantinople, who according to tradition received the revelation concerning the location of the Baptist's head at Comana.

[^390]: A historical reference to Emperor Michael III (or Constantine/Theodora) and Patriarch Ignatius receiving the Baptist's head upon its translation from Comana to Constantinople.

[^391]: In the Pochaiv print, this stanza is rubricated as a Theotokion (*Бг҃оро́диченъ*), combining praise of the Theotokos's childbearing with the Forerunner's prenatal leap and proclamation of the Lamb of God.

[^392]: The 'angel-named emperor' (царь агглоименитый) historically denotes Emperor Michael III (reigned 842–867), during whose reign the third finding and translation to Constantinople took place under Patriarch Ignatius.

[^393]: Epistle pericope 176 of 2 Corinthians is 2 Cor. 4:6–15.

[^394]: Gospel pericope 40 of Matthew is Matt. 11:2–15.

[^395]: This rubric governs the Typikon confluence when May 25 falls on the Sunday of the Holy Fathers of the First Ecumenical Council (the Sunday after Ascension, 7th Sunday of Pascha).

[^396]: That is, the Typikon chapter for the Feast of St. John the Theologian on May 8 (see fol. 27r–29v).

[^397]: Apostle Carpus of the Seventy (cf. 2 Tim. 4:13) was a disciple of the Apostle Paul and bishop of Beroea in Thrace.

[^398]: Joseph the Hymnographer (c. 816–886), author of the Canon to Apostle Carpus.

[^399]: Saint Carpus was a close companion and helper of the Apostle Paul, mentioned in 2 Tim. 4:13 ('The cloak that I left at Troas with Carpus, when thou comest, bring with thee').

[^400]: A prophetic typological allusion to Habakkuk 3:3 ('God will come from Teman, and the Holy One from mount Paran, overshadowing and densely wooded').

[^401]: Saint Therapon, Hieromartyr and Bishop of Sardis (or Cyprus), martyred under the Roman persecutions (commemorated May 27).

[^402]: The canon is authored by St. Joseph the Hymnographer (c. 816–886), one of the principal canonographers of the Byzantine liturgical tradition.

[^403]: The hymnographic imagery of a "hospitable star" (Slavonic: *гостинну тя звѣзду*) plays on the Greek etymology of the name Therapon (*Θεράπων*, "servant, attendant, healer") and the virtue of hospitality/service.

[^404]: An explicit epigraphic wordplay on the name Therapon: "bearing thy name in truth and fittingly, O martyr" (*истинно нося и подобно мучениче, твое званіе воистинну*), referring to his vocation as a true servant (*therapon*) of Christ.

[^405]: St. Nicetas the Confessor, Bishop of Chalcedon (late 8th – early 9th century), renowned for his pastoral charity, defense of holy icons under Leo the Armenian, and subsequent exile.

[^406]: In Byzantine hymnography, the patriarch Joseph is pre-eminently remembered for his chastity and temperance (*цѣломудріе*, Greek *σωφροσύνη*), having resisted the advances of Potiphar's wife (Gen. 39).

[^407]: St. Ignatius, brother and fellow-ascetic of St. Nicetas of Chalcedon, commemorated alongside him in the Byzantine synaxaria.

[^408]: An epigraphic wordplay on the name Nicetas (*Νικήτας*, from *νίκη*, victory): "as one named after victory (*побѣды яко тезоименитъ*), thou didst inherit a crown of miracles from the Crown-giver."

[^409]: An explicit theological reference to the Iconoclast controversy: by defending the venerable icon of Christ, St. Nicetas defended the reality of the Incarnation and restored the divine image in man (*еже по образу спаслъ еси*).

[^410]: Reference to St. Nicetas's heavenly patron and namesake (*тезоименитому*), the 4th-century Great Martyr Nicetas the Goth (commemorated Sept. 15).

[^411]: May 29 commemoration of the Holy Venerable Martyr Theodosia the Virgin of Constantinople (ca. 730), defender of the holy icon of Christ at the Bronze Gate (Chalke) under Emperor Leo III the Isaurian.

[^412]: "Copronymus" (dung-named) is the historical pejorative applied by iconodules to the Iconoclast Byzantine Emperor Constantine V (reigned 741–775), who ordered the removal and destruction of the icon of Christ at the Bronze Gate.

[^413]: Church Slavonic acrostic: *Ѳеодо́сїи мꙋ́нцы пою̀ сла́вꙋ* ("To the martyr Theodosia I sing praise").

[^414]: The Greek name Theodosia (Θεοδοσία) signifies "God-given" or "gift of God" (from θεός + δόσις).

[^415]: Cf. Song of Songs 1:3: "Thy name is as ointment [myrrh] poured forth."

[^416]: May 30 commemoration of Our Venerable Father Isaac (Isaacius, d. 383 or 396), founder and hegumen of the Dalmatian Monastery in Constantinople, who confronted Emperor Valens over Arianism and was later venerated as patron of the Romanov dynasty.

[^417]: Saint Isaac famously confronted the Arian Emperor Valens three times in 378 as the emperor was departing Constantinople for the Battle of Adrianople, admonishing him to reopen the Orthodox churches.

[^418]: Saint Isaac was appointed hegumen by Saint Dalmatius, founding the Dalmatian Monastery (the first attested monastery in Constantinople).

[^419]: Saint Isaac grasped the bridle of the horse of Emperor Valens as he rode out to battle, demanding that he reopen the Orthodox churches.

[^420]: Saint Isaac prophesied that Valens would be burned alive in a barn after fleeing from the battlefield, which was fulfilled after the Battle of Adrianople in 378.

[^421]: May 31 commemoration of the Holy Apostle Hermes of the Seventy (bishop of Philippopolis, mentioned in Rom. 16:14) and the Holy Martyr Hermias (Hermias of Comana, ca. 166).

[^422]: Joseph the Hymnographer (ca. 816–886), prolific Byzantine hymnographer and poet of the Studite tradition, author of numerous canons in the Menaion.

[^423]: An allusion to the Passion of the Holy Martyr Hermias of Comana (Cappadocia, martyred under Antoninus Pius, ca. 166 AD), whose tortures included having his teeth knocked out and the skin flayed from his face.

[^424]: Melodic model: *Вознесы́йся на Кре́стъ во́лею* (*Voznesyisya na Krest voleyu* / *Ὁ ὑψωθεὶς ἐν τῷ Σταυρῷ ἑκουσίως*), the celebrated Kontakion of the Exaltation of the Holy Cross.

[^425]: According to the hagiographical account, Saint Hermias was cast into a glowing fiery furnace, wherein he remained three days unburned and unharmed, praising God.

[^426]: According to the *Synaxarion*, an unnamed pagan sorcerer was hired to prepare a lethal poison for Hermias. When the poison caused the saint no harm, the sorcerer compounded an even stronger draft, which likewise had no effect; astonished by the miracle, the sorcerer renounced idolatry, confessed Christ, and was immediately beheaded, receiving martyrdom before Hermias.

[^427]: Hermias was subjected to severe bodily tortures, including the excision of his tendons and sinews.

[^428]: Melodic model: *Е́же ѡ на́съ испо́лнивъ смотре́нїе* (*Ezhe o nas ispolniv smotreniye* / *Τὴν ὑπὲρ ἡμῶν πληρώσας οἰκονομίαν*), the automelon Kontakion of the Feast of the Ascension of our Lord.

[^429]: An echo of the Nicene-Constantinopolitan Creed: *распя́таго же за ны при Понті́йстѣмъ Пила́тѣ* (*crucifixus etiam pro nobis sub Pontio Pilato* / *σταυρωθέντα τε ὑπὲρ ἡμῶν ἐπὶ Ποντίου Πιλάτου*).

[^430]: An ornate baroque woodcut tailpiece produced at the Pochaiv Lavra printshop, marking the completion of the liturgical services for the month of May.

[^431]: The catchword *Бгородичны* (*Theotokia*) at the foot of fol. 118r references the standard supplementary appendices of generic Dismissal Theotokia (*Theotokia apolytikia*) frequently appended to Menaion volumes, which were omitted or left unprinted in this copy where fol. 118v remains blank.
